Document 335: Construction rotates from infrastructure to housing and data centres (2026-11-25)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Construction rotates from infrastructure to housing and data centres (2026-11-25).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 18
Status: ok

### Page 1

#australia #construction
Construction rotates from infrastructure to housing and data
centres
By Pat Bustamante
Senior Economist, Westpac
14:36 November 25 2026 Share
Construction activity rose 0.9%qtr in Q3 and 5.8%yr. Construction activity remained solid,
driven by residential building (+3.1%qtr), non-residential building (+1.5%qtr, includes data
centres), partly offset by engineering construction (-0.9%qtr).

### Page 2

Construction activity rose 0.9%qtr in Q3 2026, taking annual growth to a strong 5.8%. The quarterly
increase was close to Westpac Economics’ 0.8%qtr forecast, but well above the 0.4%qtr market
consensus. Importantly, sizeable upward revisions to private construction lifted annual growth by
almost 2ppts. These revisions suggest the economy was somewhat stronger in 2025–26 than
previously thought, despite the same observed inflation outcomes.

What does this mean for the near-term outlook?
Overall, today’s data show construction activity continuing to expand at a solid pace, although there are
important offsetting forces beneath the headline result. Public infrastructure work remains soft and is
expected to ease further as major east-coast projects reach completion. This is being partly offset by
public non-residential construction, particularly health, schools and other social infrastructure.
At the same time, private construction remains strong. Residential construction is up 9.3% over the
year, the commercial building pipeline, which includes data centres, is around record highs, and the
renewable-energy investment pipeline remains substantial as we work towards the 82% renewable
electricity target by 2030.
Taken together, demand in the construction sector is likely to remain elevated in the near term, but its

### Page 3

composition is shifting. The pullback in public infrastructure is freeing up labour and other construction
capacity for housing, data centres, renewables and commercial projects. This rebalancing has
already been substantial, with our estimates suggesting the government footprint in the
construction industry declined by around 1.5ppts in 2025–26 (Government’s footprint plateaus as
services rise and construction retreats).
What does this mean for the National Accounts?

Today’s release is the first of a set of partials which will feed into next week’s September quarter 2026
National Accounts. Today’s numbers alone point to stronger growth in residential and new buildings
activity over the quarter and some upside for our Q3 estimate. However, with several important partial
indicators still to come, we will provide our full assessment in Friday’s GDP preview.
What about inflationary pressures and the impact of the data centre boom?

The spike in construction costs during the pandemic was largely synchronised across dwellings, non-
residential buildings and engineering construction. Today, however, cost pressures differ as demand
profiles across these segments diverge.
While rebounding in the quarter, engineering construction costs have been softer as major public
infrastructure programs wind down. In contrast, dwelling construction costs are accelerating as activity
in this segment picks up. Non-residential building costs appear to be going sideways, rather than

significantly reaccelerating, despite the strength in private investment, including data centres.

### Page 4

At 4.5%yr, aggregate construction cost growth is above what was recorded over 2018-19. This raises
questions about whether the strong demand in pockets like data centre investment are contributing to
broader inflationary pressures. For now, there is limited evidence of this happening. However,
construction cost deflators continue to firm in year-ended terms, suggesting that the disinflation
anticipated by the RBA may take longer to materialise.
How this plays out going forward depends on how much the substantial roll-off in public construction

activity makes room for the pickup in private residential and non-residential building work, particularly
data centres, and in private engineering construction associated with the renewable energy transition.
Total construction work yet to be done has risen to around 9.2% of nominal GDP in Q2 2026, compared
with an average of around 8.8% since 2022.
If that pipeline continues to build faster than public infrastructure releases capacity,
construction-sector cost pressures could prove more persistent than currently anticipated.

However, this is not our central case, as higher interest rates and ongoing cost increases are
likely to dampen private demand, while the public infrastructure pipeline continues to wind
down.

### Page 5

The detail
Residential construction outperformed, rising 3.1%qtr in Q3, following an upwardly revised 2.8%qtr
increase in Q2 2026 (previously estimated at 1.7%qtr). Growth was driven by a 3.9%qtr increase in
private residential construction, its strongest quarterly gain since June 2005, outside the COVID period.
In year-ended terms, residential construction is up 9.3%yr, the strongest growth rate since the
September quarter of 2016. This suggests the sector is responding to growing housing demand by

expanding output despite ongoing cost pressures. Some of this strength may also reflect the pullback
in public construction activity, which is freeing up labour and other resources for residential and non-
residential projects.
The strength in residential construction suggests the sector is responding more meaningfully to
Australia's housing shortage. However, with approvals likely to soften as higher interest rates bite and
demand moderates, the current pace of expansion may prove difficult to sustain.

### Page 6

Engineering construction (EC) declined 0.9%qtr in Q3 2026 but was 1.3%higher in year-ended
terms.
As foreshadowed in this month’s Market Outlook, the public infrastructure pipeline appears to have
passed its peak, as major transport projects across Australia’s east coast, particularly in NSW and
Victoria, move towards completion. Consistent with this, public engineering construction declined
3.0%year in Q3 but stabilised over the quarter to post a gain of 2.9%qtr. More broadly, the trend

remains weak, with activity falling in five of the past seven quarters.

### Page 7

In contrast, private engineering construction remains robust, up almost 5% over the year. However,
activity fell 3.7%qtr in Q3, suggesting some moderation may also be emerging in this segment. One
area of continued strength is electricity infrastructure, with the value of private work on electricity
generation and distribution rising by an estimated 10%qtr in seasonally adjusted and nominal terms,
largely reflecting renewable energy projects such as wind and solar developments.

Looking ahead, the private engineering pipeline remains supported by structural investment themes,
particularly the energy transition and nationwide rollout of data centres. However, constraints around
utility provision, grid connections and supporting infrastructure remain a key risk to the timely delivery
of these projects.
Non-residential building construction increased 1.5%qtr in Q3 2026, leaving activity 10.2% higher
over the year. Private-sector activity was mixed, declining 0.4%qtr, but remained 11% higher in year-

ended terms. Public activity also picked up sharply, rising 6.2%qtr to be 8.3% higher over the year,
partly reflecting investment in health and other facilities required to service a growing population.
Overall, non-residential construction remains strong, supported by two key investment themes: the
rollout of data centres in the private sector and increased public investment in health and other social
infrastructure, including in Queensland.

### Page 8

Although the contribution from individual projects is difficult to quantify, the surge in data centre
commencements during the second half of 2025 is likely supporting activity and should remain an
important source of construction demand over the second half of this year and beyond.

### Page 9

State detail
Construction activity increased across four of the six states in Q3, although outcomes varied
considerably.
South Australia recorded the strongest quarterly increase, rising 3.9%qtr, followed by NSW (+2.2%qtr)
and Victoria (+0.8%qtr). In contrast, activity declined in Western Australia (–1.9%qtr) and Queensland
(–1.2%qtr).
Despite the quarterly volatility, construction activity remained higher than a year ago across all states.
Tasmania recorded the strongest annual growth (+17.3%yr), followed by South Australia (+11.3%yr),

NSW (+8.1%yr), Western Australia (+6.4%yr), Victoria (+4.4%yr) and Queensland (+2.6%yr).

Document 338: COP31 Takeaways_ Collapse or reset_ (2026-11-25)

Source PDF: all report pdf/COP31 Takeaways_ Collapse or reset_ (2026-11-25).pdf

Date: 2026-11-25

### Page 1

COP31 Takeaways: Collapse or reset?
Climate diplomacy has stalled. COP31 in Antalya closed without a headline agreement, something that has not happened
since 2009. Talks on moving away from fossil fuels proved too divisive, and progress on funding for developing countries
to adapt was limited. Yet we see a necessary reset rather than a collapse. Electrification, a key step in the energy
transition, has support, and coalitions of willing countries may press ahead without waiting for the consensus on climate

Deutsche Bank
Research
Debbie Jones | Andrew Dixon
S U S T A I N A B I L I T Y  |  T H E M A T I C  R E S E A R C H  |  N O V E M B E R  2 0 2 6
10 key takeaways from COP31 (see pages 2–5 for a summary)

### Page 2

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
COP 3 1  TAK EAW AYS   |   I NTR OD U CTI ON AND  OV ER V I EW
A collapse or reset? Why did COP31 talks break down and what happens next
2
The COP process stalled this year, but a reset may be on the horizon
The UN climate summit in Antalya (Türkiye) ended on Sunday without a headline
agreement, after a draft was rejected as too weak on fossil fuels. Many are calling
the outcome a collapse of the COP process, which brings together almost 200

nations to agree shared climate action. At the same time, the science now suggests
the world will overshoot the 1.5°C global warming limit set in the Paris Agreement,
and the pressure for tougher action is growing. The outcome is a headwind to
climate diplomacy, but it also suggests to us that elements of the COP process need
to evolve to ensure compatibility with the current economic and political backdrop.
A growing bloc is no longer willing to accept weak / no language on fossil fuels

The headline agreement or “final text” is made by consensus. While this is open to a
degree of interpretation, what is clear is that a few objections can block a deal. The
EU, Norway and several vulnerable countries did just that, arguing the draft fell short
of what is needed to tackle climate change. It was the first time since 2009 that a
COP has ended without an overarching agreement. More than 130 countries backed
stronger wording on fossil fuels, up from about 80 at COP30 last year. Resistance to

the language also grew, with some oil-producing countries pushing instead for a
bigger role for carbon capture technology, which widened the divide.
Poorer countries were left disappointed on climate finance commitments
Developing countries had hoped for clarity on last year’s agreement to triple funding
for adaptation finance. Strained public finances in developed countries made
progress more challenging. The US withdrawal from the Paris Agreement, together

with cuts to its overseas climate funding, has left a gap others are struggling to fill.
Private capital will be needed to fill the gap, but the economics must work
Public money alone will not deliver the $300bn a year that developed countries
agreed at COP29 to mobilise by 2035. Having argued the draft was too weak to
address climate change, Europe will be expected to help mobilise private capital at
scale. That means policy support and steps to reduce investors' risk, so that returns

are attractive enough to draw capital in greater numbers.
Voluntary action will also need to set a new standard for progress
We expect groups of like-minded countries and voluntary plans, which will be
intertwined with COP discussions without being dependent on the formal consensus
process, to increasingly drive progress on divisive topics. Examples include several
country specific Santa Marta coalition national plans to move away from fossil fuels

and Brazil's voluntary transition roadmap. In this respect, failure to reach consensus
at COP31 may prompt the change of pace needed for countries to move forward.
Electrification offers common ground
Türkiye's voluntary “35-by-35” pledge, which aims to lift electricity's share of global
final energy use from c.23% to 35% by 2035, was the clearest common ground in
Antalya. More than 80 countries endorsed it, including China and the EU, and it drew

strong business backing. Critics note it does not explicitly require clean power, but it
encourages it, and that flexibility helped widen support while fitting energy-security
goals. We expect focus on electrification at future COPs, with attention shifting from
endorsements to delivery, such as investment in grids and the electrification of
transport and industry, and to whether more countries can be brought on board.
What happens next?
Bonn’s mid-year talks will test whether negotiators can rebuild consensus on the

divisive issues. COP32 in Addis Ababa in 2027 will then turn to delivery, with a focus
on adaptation finance (a focus for many African nations). COP33 in 2028, which
concludes the second global stocktake and thus shapes countries’ next climate plans
due in 2030, still has no host: China reportedly declined to bid and India withdrew its
offer. A swift decision matters beyond logistics. COP31 may have faltered partly
because Türkiye and Australia had less than a year to prepare, which didn’t provide

enough time to build the necessary consensus.
This report is constructed in two parts. The first (pages 2-5) sets out our top 10 takeaways
from COP31 in short form, for readers who want our high-level thoughts and observations.
The second covers each takeaway in full, followed by a look at COP next steps, and an
appendix explaining key COP terms mentioned in this note.

NO HEADLINE AGREEMENT
Consensus agreement tested; the COP
process will be forced to evolve
See page 7
– Amid questions over the effectiveness of multilateralism, COP31 delegates failed to agree on a headline agreement (i.e.,
the final cover text). This is important as the text is typically seen as a key signal for the global climate agenda. While this

outcome is a setback for the COP goal of collective climate progress, the result will force a rethink of the process.
– This result is disappointing for climate advocates and developing nations, but the outcome is also acknowledgement that
the current process is not delivering at the pace required to address the now anticipated temperature overshoot. We still
see a role for consensus decision making, but it now won’t be relied upon. Instead, we expect to see greater focus on

voluntary action and “coalitions of the willing”, whereby like-minded countries seek action on divisive issues.
MORE SUPPORT FOR FOSSIL FUEL
TRANSITION MET WITH RESISTANCE
Coalitions of the willing are emerging
and building transition roadmaps
See page 8
– Despite the lack of agreement on the final cover text, we took away from COP that there is a notable faction of support
for a transition away from fossil fuels. This is something to watch. Reports suggest that more than 130 countries backed

or would not object to stronger fossil fuel language, up from more than 80 at COP30. Energy security worries and
heightened concern over rising temperatures likely played a role in this shift.
– Away from the formal negotiations, Brazil’s COP30 presidency presented its voluntary global roadmap for transitioning
away from fossil fuels. While not binding, notable countries including India and Nigeria are reportedly welcoming it.
France, Germany, Finland, Denmark and the Netherlands have also published national transition roadmaps. More could

follow ahead of the second voluntary Santa Marta coalition meeting next July, co-hosted by Tuvalu and Ireland. This will
be an early test of whether high-level support translates into action, policy and investment.
STRAINED CLIMATE FINANCE
Developing countries are frustrated
with the COP process, while developed
countries are constrained
See pages 9–10
– Climate finance, including adaptation, was the priority for most developing countries at COP31, but progress was limited.

A key ask was clearer language defining the pledge to triple adaptation finance by 2035, agreed last year in Belém
without a headline figure or baseline year. An early call to solidify this as a $120bn annual target was dropped from later
drafts, though this was always the most likely outcome given the EU had ruled out new pledges.
– The shortfall in climate finance delivery against existing pledges is most concerning to the Global South, where climate

impacts are most severe and the capacity to fund adaptation is weakest. Developing countries often can’t meet their
Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) goals to lower emissions without better access to finance, and this extends
beyond the amount available, with accessibility and conditions (particularly around debt burdens) a barrier.
– There was a breakthrough on technical aspects around refining a set of 59 indicators to measure adaptation progress

under the Global Goal on Adaptation. Once fully detailed, these indicators will provide important signals as to the areas
where finance for adaptation projects is most urgently required.

IPCC TIMELINE IN QUESTION
No agreed timeline for the reports key
to the 2028 global stocktake
See page 12
– A recurring concern among attendees was a potential delay to the IPCC’s next assessment reports, for two reasons. First,
the reports are key to informing the second global stocktake, concluding in 2028. Second, our discussions suggest that

climate deterioration is happening faster than initially expected, thus delay risks decisions resting on out-of-date science.
– Even if there is agreement to produce the IPCC report on time, it may not be fully accurate, as the timeline may limit
contributions from scientists in developing countries, such as Kenya, who are struggling to secure the funding and
capacity needed to contribute within the shorter timeframe.
TRADE TENSIONS CONTINUE
Pushback against the EU’s CBAM and

other unilateral trade measures
See page 13
– Developing nations, including China, India and the African Group, pushed back against unilateral climate-related trade
measures, with the EU’s Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM) a particular target. We expect trade to remain a
source of friction in climate diplomacy, while cleaner producers should gain a global trade advantage.
ELECTRIFICATION
The clearest common ground, with 80+
countries backing 35-by-35

See page 14
– Electrification was the clearest area of common ground in Antalya. The voluntary “35-by-35” pledge aims to raise
electricity’s share of global final energy use from about 23% today to 35% by 2035, and was endorsed by more than 80
countries, including China and the EU, with strong business support. Current and stated policies point to only 28–29% by
2035, so a gap remains.
– While aligned with broader energy security goals, the electrification pledge drew criticism for not requiring clean energy.

We viewed the ambiguity as a way to facilitate broader agreement and grow the infrastructure needed for a more
meaningful shift away from fossil fuels. We expect continued building on this goal at future COP events.
PRESSURE ON EUROPE
Having rejected the cover text, Europe
will be expected to lead action
See page 11
04
– Exiting COP31, we expect more diplomatic pressure on the European nations to back up their stance that the COP31
draft text did not go far enough to address climate change. The EU, UK, Norway and Switzerland all either formally

rejected or voiced opposition to the draft text. Countries choosing to walk away will be expected to show how they can
deliver the ambition they say the text lacked.
– Public finance constraints (a point of contention and a limiting factor for ambition at this year’s COP) will mean taking
steps to enable private capital, as well as driving the regulation needed to mobilise it. At issue is that investors face
limited transparency, hard-to-assess risk and a high cost of capital, which guarantees and other risk-sharing tools could

help reduce. As one participant noted, private finance is being asked to show up at COP. They need a reason to do so.

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
5
Antalya - 10 takeaways from COP31
09
10
08
COP 3 1  TAK EAW AYS  |  TOP  1 0  (3  o f 3 )
AI & CLIMATE
A missed opportunity
See page 15
– AI was a missed opportunity. While the power demands of data centres were acknowledged as a driver of increased
energy demand, discussion of AI’s role in advancing climate goals was surprisingly limited: just 2% of the more than 4,000
Blue Zone events we analysed had a notable focus on AI. Given the strong presence of climate technology at COP, we had

expected AI to feature far more prominently.
– The Antalya AI Pledge drew a muted response with only seven governments among c.80 backers. Major US AI firms
stayed away, though Alibaba signed up. Launched by the Presidency with the ITU, it serves as a voluntary framework
committing signatories to measurable action to use AI for climate goals while curbing its own energy and resource
footprint. As the focus on data centres increases, we expect this topic to feature more prominently at COP.

US WITHDRAWAL HAD AN IMPACT, BUT
DEMOCRATS SHOW UP
The US exit has left a clear dent, but
Democratic governors express
optimism
See pages 16-17
– The Trump administration sent no official delegation for a second year. After exiting the Paris Agreement, the US is set to
become the first country to leave the UNFCCC, the treaty that underpins global climate diplomacy. More than 120 US
state, local and business leaders attended COP, but as observers without a seat at the negotiating table. This absence

was felt across negotiating rooms, with observers highlighting the key role previously played by the US in bridging
divides. For the next few years at least, this dynamic is set to continue.
– The Democrats showed up with a message of optimism. The America Is All In coalition held several panels at COP,
highlighting that following the midterms, Democratic governors will lead states home to roughly three-quarters of the US
population, supporting the view that state-level policy will remain a key driver of US clean energy and industrial

investment. While we tend to agree, the magnitude of the benefit isn’t entirely clear.
NATURE, CARBON MARKETS & OCEANS
Future progress needed, but some
action was notable
See page 18
– Warming oceans and rising sea levels are a growing economic risk, damaging coastal infrastructure and fisheries and
raising insurance costs. COP31 delivered momentum but no negotiated ocean outcome. Funding was modest, including a
CAD$14mn commitment from Canada to the Ocean Risk and Resilience Action Alliance.

– Forest finance received a boost, with Canada pledging up to $392.5mn to the Tropical Forest Forever Facility (TFFF),
launched at COP30, taking total sponsor pledges to $8.3bn. The TFFF matters because it pays countries to keep tropical
forests standing, making protection more valuable than clearing. This voluntary effort was launched by Brazil at COP30.
– Carbon markets inched forward as attention turned to putting Article 6, the Paris Agreement’s rulebook for trading

emissions cuts between countries, into practice. The first version of the international Article 6.2 registry went live, and
Norway agreed to buy up to 12 MtCO₂e of credits from Indonesia. On 12 November, 14 governments signed the Antalya
Statement on Mobilising Private Finance for Climate Action, with a playbook of eight policy levers to grow corporate
demand for high-integrity credits. Corporates and investors still stress the need for robust credits that deliver the

promised emissions reductions and limit greenwashing risk.

### Page 7

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
TAK EAW AY 1   |   MU L TI L ATER AL I S M
No cover text: COP31 delegates fail to agree, testing multilateralism and the consensus model
7
Amid questions over the effectiveness of multilateralism, COP31 delegates failed to agree the final cover text, typically seen as a key signal for the global climate agenda. While this no
doubt sets back timelines for near-term implementation of agreed climate goals, it will likely force the COP process to evolve. This result is disappointing for climate advocates, but the

outcome is also acknowledgement that the current process is not delivering action at the pace required to address the temperature overshoot. We don’t think multilateralism as part
of the COP process is dead, but it now won’t be relied upon. Instead, we expect to see greater focus on “coalitions of the willing”, whereby like-minded countries seek progress on
divisive issues.
Formal objections by several parties to the cover text proposed at COP31 meant

consensus could not be achieved
Decision making at COP requires consensus among parties. What this means in practice
is open to interpretation, particularly whether objection from a single country can block
an agreement. Control of proceedings rests with the COP President. At COP31, however,
formal objections from several parties, including the EU, Vanuatu, Panama and Norway,
meant the consensus test was clearly not satisfied. With the Presidency confirming no

further text would follow, COP31 ended on 22 November without a cover agreement –
the first time since COP15 in Copenhagen in 2009.
Multilateralism has produced landmark results, but the backdrop has changed
The Paris Agreement (COP21 in 2015, which set the goal of limiting warming to well
below 2°C while pursuing 1.5°C) and the UAE Consensus (COP28 in 2023, agreement to
“transition away from fossil fuels”) were remarkable achievements, agreed by almost 200

nations. Since then, the world has changed. With delivery requiring financial backing,
climate change has become more politically fractious, as geopolitical tensions, trade
disputes, energy-security concerns and cost-of-living pressures have pushed
governments to prioritise domestic competitiveness over collective action. The US
withdrawal from the Paris Agreement has further weakened the multilateral push.
Pre-COP diplomacy was challenged given the short time frame

Some contrast Antalya with Dubai, where the COP28 presidency spent much of the year
building consensus before the summit. Some felt Australia was seen as the junior
partner (despite leading the formal negotiations), which created confusion. The Türkiye–
Australia split was only agreed at COP30 in November 2025, leaving under a year to
prepare. The lateness and dual structure may also have blurred accountability during the
process.
Looking ahead to next year, the mid-year talks in Bonn and COP32 in Addis Ababa

will seek to overcome the divides which proved too large to bridge at COP31
With the African Group frustrated that adaptation finance was overshadowed by the
fossil fuel debate, we expect a more concrete definition of the pledge to triple adaptation
finance to be a clear priority for the Ethiopian presidency. Meanwhile, the COP33 host for
2028 remains unclear, after China reportedly decided not to bid. This matters, as COP33
will conclude the Paris Agreement’s second Global Stocktake.

Multilateralism as part of the COP process will continue, but we expect greater
emphasis on “coalitions of the willing”
Voluntary structures are arguably more suitable for achieving certain goals when
resistance to climate objectives is strong. The new Santa Marta coalition is a clear
example, with 50+ countries advancing fossil fuel transition planning after COP30 in
Belém failed to include such language. Because it sits outside the formal consensus

process, members can move forward on language that could not be agreed in Antalya.
While the cover text failure will dominate the media headlines, we would highlight
some notable announcements outside the formal negotiations
– Non-CO₂ GHGs: $480mn+ of new philanthropic funding at the leaders’ methane
summit; 35 countries signed Germany’s pledge to cut power-sector emissions of
sulphur hexafluoride (SF6).
– Clean energy: Türkiye announced a $15bn clean energy deal with China. Amazon

announced $7bn of additional investment in Indian renewables by 2030 and a 65%
cut in the carbon intensity of its electricity by 2030 (vs 2019).
– Industry: Antalya Green Industrialisation Compact (aims to drive investment in cleaner
steel and cement) and UNIDO’s Global Green Industrialization Agenda both launched.

### Page 8

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
TAK EAW AY 2   |   F OS S I L  F U EL S
The fossil fuel dispute blocked agreement, but support for a transition is growing
8
The fossil fuel language dispute proved too big to overcome, but there is notable support for a transition away from fossil fuels. This is something to watch. Reports suggest that more
than 130 countries backed stronger fossil fuel language, up from more than 80 at COP30. In addition, Brazil’s COP30 presidency presented its voluntary roadmap for transitioning away

from fossil fuels at COP31, outside the negotiations. It invited countries to set national plans by 2028, with the main cuts to coal, oil and gas expected after 2031. While not binding,
countries including India and Nigeria are reportedly welcoming it. Furthermore, a handful of European countries, including France, Germany, Finland, Denmark and the Netherlands,
have published national roadmaps, while Brazil is preparing its own. More could follow ahead of the second Santa Marta meeting next July, co-hosted by Tuvalu and Ireland.

The UAE Consensus pledge to “transition away from fossil fuels” was a historic
commitment, yet building on it has been a point of contention ever since
The UAE Consensus was agreed at COP28 (2023), and represented the first call to
“transition away from fossil fuels” in a COP final text. However, attempts to take this
pledge further or even simply repeat it have been the source of major disagreement at
every COP since. Talks last year in Belém came close to collapse over this issue, and this

time in Antalya proved to be a tipping point for nations in favour of stronger climate
action.
With a significant increase in backing for stronger fossil fuel transition language at
COP31, supporting nations chose to make a stand
Reports suggest that more than 130 countries backed (or would not object to) stronger
fossil fuel language, up from more than 80 at COP30, with this significant numerical shift
in favour of transition language likely a result of increased concern over fossil fuel

dependencies, scientific confirmation of a temperature overshoot, and increased
organisation among nations in favour of such an outcome.
A group of fossil fuel producing nations maintained their red line that a fossil fuel
mention was not acceptable
While an earlier draft of the cover decision did include a reference to action to phase out
fossil fuels, this was removed. Nations including Saudi Arabia and Russia were opposed
to any such mention, with the latter claiming that cutting fossil fuel production faster

than demand could result in “fuel poverty”. Nations led by the EU, however, refused to
alter their positions, with German Environment Minister Schneider stating “a step
backwards compared with previous climate-protection decisions is the last thing
Germany can afford”. In the end, neither faction was willing to give ground.
A dispute over the inclusion in the cover decision of language on carbon capture,
utilisation and storage technologies ran parallel to the broader fossil fuel debate

These technologies (known as CCUS) capture CO2 before it reaches the atmosphere and
store it underground or use it in other processes. At COP31, the Arab Group (led by
Saudi Arabia) pushed strongly for their inclusion in the cover decision, with one
representative stating “it makes no sense that concrete solutions will be taken off the
table”. Other nations, however, pushed back, with the EU claiming that the inclusion of
CCUS would send a “signal contrary to the need of cutting emissions”. Some

stakeholders opposed it on the grounds that the technology has yet to be proven as
effective / viable on a larger scale.
The IPCC does recognise the role of carbon capture in lowering net emissions
With or without a place in the COP process, the technology is expanding. It is increasingly
seen as a pathway for oil-producing nations that also have the capital to invest in it. In
our view, this is likely another area where different factions will break off and pursue

their own goals away from the formal COP process.
Geoengineering was another technological solution discussed
Geoengineering aims to limit the impact of rising temperatures rather than directly
reduce emissions. One example is stratospheric aerosol injection, which would release
reflective particles into the upper atmosphere to mimic the cooling effect of large
volcanic eruptions. However, the technique remains largely untested and the
conversation is not mainstream at COP, but could gain more attention as adaptation

costs swell.  In Singapore, water agency PUB and US start-up Equatic have built Equatic-1
in Tuas, set to be the world’s largest facility for removing CO2 from the ocean.

### Page 9

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
TAK EAW AY 3   |   CL I MATE & AD AP TATI ON F I NANCE
Climate finance delivery fell short, with adaptation finance overshadowed
9
Climate finance, including adaptation, was the priority for most developing countries at COP31, but progress was limited. The shortfall is felt most in the Global South, where impacts
are severe and capacity is weakest. The key ask was clearer language on the Belém pledge to triple adaptation finance by 2035, lacking a figure or baseline. An early draft’s $120bn

target was dropped, and the EU ruled out new pledges, preferring delivery of existing ones. The main step was the Adaptation Fund’s switch to serving only the Paris Agreement,
though its value hinges on Article 6.4 markets scaling. With public finance constrained, private capital is needed, with Europe expected to support incentives to mobilise it.
Adaptation finance was a priority for the Global South, but progress fell short of
their demands
Sentiment from the Chair of the African Group of Negotiators after talks collapsed

reflected this. They claimed that the fossil fuel dispute was “over-hyped”, adding that this
meant their demands for greater focus on adaptation finance were “overshadowed”.
While an initial draft text contained reference to a $120bn annual adaptation target for
2035 (which would have reflected a tripling of the previous $40bn annual goal for 2025),
this did not appear in subsequent versions (some sources suggested that France instead
pushed for a $60bn figure, which would represent a tripling vs. a 2019 baseline). The

significance of an adaptation-specific goal (as part of the broader $1.3tn climate finance
ambition) lies in the fact that adaptation projects often lack a clear source of returns,
thus are much more dependent on public financing relative to mitigation projects.
Defining the adaptation tripling pledge was expected to be challenging
Prior to the conference, the EU ruled out making any new finance pledges, and this
sentiment was repeated by high-level EU officials at the event. They argued that the

focus should be on delivering against existing pledges, while pointing out the significant
climate finance contributions already made by the EU. With clarity on the adaptation
commitment a key priority for the African Group of Negotiators, this will likely be a focus
next year at COP32 in Addis Ababa.
There was agreement to move forward refining the adaptation indicators, which
will help identify where finance is most needed
Last year at COP30, countries agreed on a set of 59 adaptation indicators to measure

progress under the Global Goal on Adaptation. At COP31, this process moved forward
with an agreement over the composition of technical committees to refine these
indicators. Completing these indicators will provide standardized metrics to support
identification of adaptation finance needs.
A technical breakthrough on the Adaptation Fund at COP31 could unlock more
adaptation finance via Article 6.4 carbon markets, if they can scale
Transitioning the Adaptation Fund (which provides developing countries with finance for

adaptation projects) to exclusively serve the Paris Agreement (having previously served
both the Paris Agreement and the now largely superseded Kyoto Protocol), was a
positive outcome at COP31. The significance of this development lies in the fact that it
enables the fund to receive a mandatory 5% share of proceeds from carbon credit
trading conducted via a centralised UNFCCC mechanism under Article 6.4 of the Paris
Agreement. At present, however, this market remains small, meaning the long-term

significance of this breakthrough will depend on the scaling of trading under Article 6.4.
Continued public financing constraints will mean a greater role for the private
sector; yet there are clear barriers to overcome.
Current geopolitical and economic realities mean that public finance from developed
countries will remain constrained. Private capital will therefore need to be leaned upon if
the world is to limit (and reverse) the projected overshoot of 1.5°C, which will require

government support, transparency and de-risking to ensure finance is accessible to
developing nations. Discussions at COP31 highlighted examples where this is working in
bankable areas such as grids and industrial decarbonisation with long-term offtake,
backed by World Bank Group guarantees and first-loss capital that helped mobilise
c.$112bn of private finance last fiscal year (covers broader mobilization not just for
climate projects). However, only c.$3bn reached low-income countries; reflecting

sentiment that much of climate finance for developing countries flows to middle-income
nations. Adaptation is less mature, lacking standardised ways to monetise avoided
losses, and project transparency remains an issue. These challenges are compounded by
gaps between investor requirements and developing country governments’ ability to
meet them, particularly in capacity constrained nations.

### Page 10

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
TAK EAW AY 3   |   CL I MATE & AD AP TATI ON F I NANCE
COP31 delivered little on climate finance, so COP32 becomes the delivery test
COP31 left the climate finance question largely unanswered. Talks on who should pay stalled and a proposed $120bn target for adaptation funding was dropped. New public pledges fell by
more than 70% in 2026 to about $11.7bn a year, with the EU ruling out fresh pledges. We expect reaching the $300bn and $1.3tn-a-year goals for 2035 will depend on private capital and

development bank reform. This slide sets out where each goal stands and the hurdles for COP32.

Are the NCQG and IHLEG aligned? IHLEG’s $1.3tn external-finance

and new public pledges in 2026 are down 70%+ to ~$11.7bn a year.
Adaptation finance
After COP31: the tripling pledge still has no baseline or target
Context: COP30 called for adaptation finance to at least triple by
2035, yet it did not specify a base year or a target figure (some have
suggested $120bn, i.e. 3x the 2025 goal).
At COP31: An early draft set $120bn a year by 2035, but it was

dropped from later texts; France reportedly proposed a 2019
baseline (~$60bn). Neither a baseline nor a target was agreed,
against UNEP-estimated needs of ~$305bn a year.
Loss and Damage Fund
After COP31: requests far exceed funds; replenishment starts in 2027
Why it matters: The Fund is the only dedicated channel for
irreversible climate impacts, yet it has no annual funding target and
resources remain far below demand.
At COP31: 176 funding requests from 119 countries total $2.8bn,

against a $342m first-round allocation (plus $21.6m set aside for a
Nepal flood pilot). COP31 only noted the shortfall and urged pledges
be converted into payments. First replenishment planned for 2027.

Hurdles to these
targets
Grants vs loans
Developing countries want grants (especially for
adaptation) and burden-sharing; developed countries
favour loans, blended finance and a wider contributor
base.
Pressure on development banks
The US is pushing MDBs (e.g. the World Bank) to drop
their climate focus, according to the World Resources
Institute (WRI). Developing countries want MDB reform
to deliver more investment and faster disbursement.
Competing budget pressures

Donors are diverting aid to defence and domestic
priorities. New public pledges are down 70%+ in 2026;
and none is expected from the US.

The IHLEG estimates ~½ the $1.3tn could come from
private sources. Private capital is looking for regulation,

### Page 11

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
TAK EAW AY 4   |   P R ES S U R E ON EU R OP E
Europe will be expected to show how ambition can be delivered given calls for greater ambition
11
GDP, Emissions and UNFCCC Classification by Country
(Wealthier Non-Annex I countries are labelled, as is the USA)
Source: World Bank, EDGAR, Deutsche Bank Research
Exiting COP31, there will be more pressure on the European nations to back up their stance that the COP31 final draft text (i.e., headline agreement) did not go far enough to address

climate change. The EU, UK, Norway and Switzerland all either formally rejected or voiced opposition to the draft text. Countries choosing to walk away will be expected to show how
they could deliver the ambition they say the text lacked, including finance for developing countries. With public finance constrained (a point of contention and driver of constraint at
this year’s COP), this means enabling private capital and driving the regulation needed to mobilise it.

The collapse of COP31 cover text negotiations should not be viewed as a shock in
isolation; arguably, this outcome has been building up over recent COPs.
Last year at COP30, EU negotiators reportedly considered walking away from talks over
dissatisfaction regarding the lack of fossil fuel language, yet they ultimately decided to accept
the deal after receiving limited concessions, with a broader sense among EU figures at the
time that it was better to keep the process moving forwards as opposed to collapsing it

entirely. However, post-COP30 discussions among EU members reportedly included a push
from some for clearer guidance on when to walk away, meaning COP31’s outcome was seen
as possible.
Having rejected the final cover text on the grounds that it was not ambitious enough,
Europe will face pressure to show how to deliver these stronger outcomes
By taking a hard line on including fossil fuel transition language in the text, Europe will be
expected to support the development of incentives and frameworks that enable more

ambitious outcomes, particularly with respect to incentivising private capital. The role of
multilateral development banks will likely be a focus, while guarantees and risk-sharing are
viewed as some of the most effective methods of scaling up private finance involvement.
Reports that China would bid to host COP33 proved false
China remains the world’s largest emitter, accounting for around a third of global emissions,
yet it has made considerable progress, often highlighted in detail at its COP pavilion. Its CO2

emissions have been broadly flat or falling since early 2024, driven by record growth in wind
and solar. China clearly wants the net zero transition to move forward, given its significant
investment in climate technology, on which the rest of the world, including Europe and the
Global South, is increasingly reliant. Notably, however, it decided not to bid to host COP33 in
2028, despite hopes from some that such an event would help fill the leadership gap left by

the US.

No agreement on the timeline for the IPCC reports key to informing the 2028 stocktake

12
A recurring concern among attendees was a potential delay to the IPCC’s next assessment reports. The concern was twofold. First, the reports are needed to inform the second global

stocktake in 2028. Second, our discussions suggest there is concern that climate deterioration is happening faster than initially expected, thus delay risks decisions resting on out-of-
date science. Even if there is agreement to produce the reports in time, there is concern that it can’t be fully accurate, as the timeline may limit contributions from scientists in
developing countries, who are struggling to secure the funding and capacity needed to contribute within the shorter timeframe.

IPCC report timelines were a point of contention at COP31 discussions
The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) is the UN body for assessing the scientific
evidence on climate change, and its assessment reports provide the scientific basis for climate
negotiations. The IPCC produces reports in 3 areas, namely 1) Physical Science, 2) Impacts,
Adaptation and Vulnerability, and 3) Mitigation, with the last such reports published prior to the

conclusion of the first global stocktake (which measures progress towards goals set out in the
Paris Agreement and informs the next round of national climate plans, known as NDCs) at COP28
in 2023. Coming into COP31, countries were aiming to agree on the timing for these reports
(having failed to reach agreement at previous IPCC meetings), such that they could be signed off at
the IPCC’s next meeting in December.
Europe pushed for faster IPCC reports, while other nations favoured delay

Typically, the IPCC reports have operated on a 5–7 year cycle, but the exact timings require
agreement among IPCC members. At COP31, European diplomats pushed for a trajectory that
would allow the next reports to inform the second global stocktake, which opened at COP31 and is
due to conclude at COP33 in 2028. Some nations favoured a longer timeline, with Kenya arguing
that scientists from developing countries needed more time to contribute, while India and Saudi

Arabia were also said to favour a delay. Some stakeholders at COP31 suggested the reports will
likely paint an alarming picture of the climate, and publishing them before the stocktake would
add pressure for stronger action.
Discussions will reconvene at December’s IPCC meetings
There is concern that agreement will not be reached in time. The longer the impasse continues,
the less time scientists have to prepare for publication. The IPCC Chair has previously been quoted

as stating that agreement must be reached by the end of 2026 if the reports are to be ready to
contribute to the second stocktake. Alongside (and somewhat linked to) disagreement over the
report timelines, nations will need to reach agreement on the IPCC’s budget for the remainder of
the current cycle.
IPCC reports and the global stocktake: key dates

stocktake

next round of global climate plans.

stocktakes every five years, unless parties agree otherwise.

change. This gave time for the findings of the 3 reports to contribute to the
first stocktake’s outcomes.

TAK EAW AY 6   |   TR AD E
Trade is still a point of contention

13
Cumulative value of EU imports from CBAM sectors by market (2015-24)
Source: BNEF, Deutsche Bank Research.
Note: Chart displays nominal value of cumulative imports to the EU by source markets.
Developing nations, including China, India and the African Group, pushed back against unilateral climate-related trade measures, with the EU’s Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism
(CBAM) a particular target. We expect trade to remain a source of friction in climate diplomacy, while cleaner producers should gain a global trade advantage.

Several nations have sought to bring trade into climate discussions at COP
The African Group negotiating bloc submitted a request prior to the event to have an item on
climate-related trade measures formally added to the COP31 agenda. The attempt was not
successful. Meanwhile, both India and China came into COP31 promising to use the event to push
back against climate-related trade mechanisms, with the EU’s Carbon Border Adjustment
Mechanism (CBAM) a particular point of contention. Developed countries continue to defend such

measures, arguing that they are necessary to level the playing field for domestic firms in cases
where foreign competitors face lower environment-related compliance costs relative to domestic
industries.
At COP30, countries agreed to create a separate space away from COP to discuss climate-
related trade measures, yet this did not stop trade entering discussions
A senior member of China’s delegation described the new platform as “not enough”, calling for

stronger cooperation on measures including defining product carbon footprints and traceability of
agricultural goods to prevent deforestation. They also added that EU-China climate relations had
been damaged by trade tensions. Saudi Arabia and Russia also raised trade during COP31
discussions. A call in the first draft of the cover text “to phase out unilateral climate-related and
trade-restrictive measures” by the end of 2027 had no prospect of success, yet it reflects the desire

of developing countries to continue raising this topic at COP.
In some negotiating tracks, disputes over trade contributed to the collapse of discussions
In a sign of how prevalent trade was in Antalya, an EU official claimed that there was “no room” at
the talks where trade was not raised, describing this as “not helpful”. Indeed, trade was also cited
as the factor behind the collapse of COP31 negotiations on climate change and agriculture, after

some parties demanded that the talks discuss the EU’s CBAM due to claims over its impacts on
fertilisers.

### Page 14

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
There was notable support for electrification goals
The COP Presidency positioned a new “35-by-35” electrification goal as a headline item
for COP31. The goal seeks to raise the share of electricity in global final energy use to
35% by 2035 (vs. c.23% currently) and was generally well received in Antalya, with more
than 80 countries reportedly endorsing it, including China and the EU.
No clean energy requirement drew scrutiny, but was a pragmatic step

As we highlighted in our COP31 preview, the lack of reference to clean energy drew
scrutiny, with concerns that the pledge would fail to deliver climate benefits if fossil fuels
were used to generate the additional electricity. Despite the EU’s eventual endorsement
of “35-by-35”, some member states were reportedly reluctant initially to sign up for this
reason. While we acknowledge these concerns, the pledge could also be viewed as a
well-crafted initiative that addresses energy security while building the infrastructure

needed for significant emissions reductions, even if not immediately. The absence of a
clean energy requirement (though it was implied) was also seen by some as a pragmatic
step that enabled wider participation, as reflected in the high number of nations
pledging support at COP31.
Despite the lack of a clean energy requirement, some countries still opposed the
inclusion of the “35-by-35” goal in the final cover decision
Some developing nations were against its inclusion on the grounds that they viewed it as

potentially restricting development choices. There was also opposition from some of
these countries against the imposition of top-down sectoral targets which do not make
allowances for varying national circumstances. Moreover, while the emphasis of the COP
Presidency on the initiative was likely a key factor behind why it received strong support,
some nations felt that this focus took away from other priorities at the event.

TAK EAW AY 7   |   EL ECTR I F I CATI ON
Electrification was the clearest common ground, with 80+ countries backing the “35-by-35” goal
14
Electrification was the clearest area of common ground in Antalya. The voluntary “35-by-35” pledge, which aims to raise electricity’s share of global final energy use to 35% by 2035, was
endorsed by more than 80 countries, including China and the EU, and drew strong business support. The pledge does not formally require clean energy, which drew criticism.

However, we viewed the ambiguity as a way to facilitate broader agreement and grow the infrastructure needed for a more meaningful shift away from fossil fuels. The effort also
aligned with broader energy security goals. We expect continued focus on this topic at future COP events.
Business engagement on electrification at COP31 was high
This was evident in the COP31 Electrification Delivery Package, which sought to highlight
billions in investment to support areas including grids and the electrification of key

sectors such as transport and industry. The UN highlighted that more than 260
businesses with more than $4tn in annual revenue “announced or profiled” actions in
support of these objectives at COP31. Meanwhile, the Utilities for Net Zero Alliance
(UNEZA) announced combined 2026 annual investment plans across grids, renewables
and storage of at least $156bn, a 5% increase on 2025 and a 35% rise vs. 2024.

### Page 15

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
AI was a missed opportunity, with just 2% of Blue Zone events focused on it
15
AI was a missed opportunity. While the power demands of data centres were acknowledged as a driver of increased energy demand, discussion of the role AI could play in advancing
climate goals was surprisingly limited. Beyond the formal negotiations, COP is also a major educational and climate technology forum, and we had expected the potential for AI to help

accelerate progress towards net zero to feature far more prominently. The Presidency’s Antalya AI Pledge was a welcome step, but drew support from only seven governments, while
support from major AI companies was also limited.
AI-related discussions weren’t as prevalent as we expected
It is unclear whether this reflects fundamental uncertainty among companies about how to scale
AI sustainably or a lack of understanding of how powerful AI could be in solving climate challenges.

In our view, it is likely a combination of both, making this a missed opportunity for COP to educate
companies and governments. That is not to say there weren’t some great AI panels. At one AI
innovation panel we attended, McKinsey noted that AI sales forecasting can help retailers match
ordering to demand and cut avoidable food waste, reducing emissions (roughly a third of food is
never eaten) and delivering monetary savings.
Only 2% of COP31 Blue Zone events featured a notable focus on AI

As a check against our COP experience, we analysed the focus of more than 4,000 panels and
events listed as being held in the Blue Zone at COP31 (those we could gather from the COP31
programme available on the official website). Our AI driven analysis assessed the focus of each
session and determined a maximum of 3 focus topics. Our analysis showed that AI had the lowest
focus of any of the listed topics, with just 2% of COP31 Blue Zone events having a notable focus on

AI. As expected, climate finance and investment was the most frequently addressed topic.
The COP31 Presidency did put AI on the Action Agenda
Türkiye launched the Antalya AI Pledge on 16 November, recognising AI both as a tool for climate
action and a growing source of environmental pressure. Developed with the ITU, this voluntary
framework asks signatories to select measurable commitments, such as powering AI infrastructure
with low- and zero-emission energy and reporting AI’s environmental footprint. By 19 November,

only seven governments had endorsed it, with little support from major AI players, Alibaba being
the notable exception. This could reflect concern that AI will challenge firms’ near-term emission
commitments, especially for tech companies. Here we would highlight a conversation we had with
a leading Chinese solar panel company. When asked how China would handle sustainability issues
from the expected rapid build-out of data centres, an attendee we spoke to said matter of factly:

“China will make data centres sustainable”.

### Page 16

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
TAK EAW AY 9   |   U NI TED  S TATES
The US federal absence weighs on COP, but the midterms boost state-level momentum
16
The absence of the US federal government under the second Trump administration has had a notable negative impact on negotiations and signaling around financing. The Democrats
continue to show up at COP, this year with a message of optimism following the midterm election results. The America Is All In coalition held several panels at COP highlighting climate

commitments and objectives. Democratic governors showed up to remind COP attendees that roughly three-quarters of the US population now have Democratic governors,
strengthening the case that state-level policy will be a key driver of US clean energy and industrial investment. While we tend to agree, the magnitude of the benefit isn’t entirely clear.
The US once again had no federal presence at COP
Explaining the decision to stay away for a second straight COP, a spokesperson for the federal

administration said it was focused on expanding “affordable, reliable, secure energy sources”. This
also meant the US had no formal negotiating presence, though the climate pivot under the second
Trump administration potentially emboldened nations opposed to faster transition timelines.
US State representation and companies did come to Antalya
At the America Is All In pavilion we attended the US Climate Alliance panel “Undaunted”, featuring
Governors Michelle Lujan Grisham (New Mexico) and Tim Walz (Minnesota). Key messages:

– States retain sufficient regulatory authority, economic influence and private sector partnerships
to keep advancing clean energy, industrial decarbonisation and climate resilience, with member
states of the alliance having reduced net greenhouse gas emissions by nearly a quarter
between 2005 and 2024 while also growing their economies by 37%.
– Clean electricity was presented as an industrial competitiveness issue, with manufacturers
demanding reliable access to clean power when deciding where to locate production.

– Affordability and employment were recurring themes, with both governors stressing that
climate policy must deliver visible economic benefits.
– Methane was a focus. New Mexico argued it has combined rising oil and gas output with
stringent methane standards and super-emitter monitoring, resulting in lower emissions.
Governor Grisham signalled hopes of encouraging Texas, which has just elected Democrat Gina
Hinojosa as governor, to adopt similar rules.

– Governor Walz of Minnesota criticised companies that publicly support climate action but locate
manufacturing in states with weaker labour and environmental policies.
– Consistent with our wider observations (see takeaway 8), AI was notably absent from the
discussion, with data centres mentioned only in passing.

In the US (and abroad), the level of alarm over global warming is inching higher
As part of our monthly Climate & Sustainability Survey, we ask consumers in the UK, US, Germany, France, Spain and Italy for their views on global warming. In Q3 2026, we recorded a notable
rebound in alarm regarding the pace of global warming, particularly in the UK and Europe following a summer of climate extremes. The level of alarm had begun to dip globally post the 2024 US

presidential election in November.

### Page 18

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
TAK EAW AY 1 0   |   NATU R E, CAR BON MAR K ETS  & OCEANS
Continued scientist alarm over nature and ocean health raises the importance of scaling financing mechanisms
18
Nature and the oceans returned to the agenda at COP31, a month after the UN’s biodiversity summit (CBD COP17) found most 2030 targets off track. Scientists warned of record
ocean heat, and with an overshoot of 1.5°C looking ever more likely, the case for scaling up finance is growing stronger. Canada’s pledge of up to $392.5mn to the Tropical Forest

Forever Facility (TFFF), launched at COP30, gave forest finance a lift, while Brazil presented a deforestation roadmap outside the formal talks. In carbon markets, the focus has moved
from writing the Article 6 rules to making them work. Buyers want high-integrity credits, governments want faster payments. In our view, buyer confidence must come first.
Scientists again warned of concerns over the health of oceans and nature
The UN held a dedicated biodiversity summit (CBD COP17) in Armenia last month. It

found that progress towards most of the 2030 targets under the Kunming-Montreal
Biodiversity Framework has been insufficient. Scientists made similar points at COP31.
The WMO’s 2026 “State of the Climate” report, published during the summit, said ocean
heat content in the upper 2,000 metres reached a record in 2025, with preliminary data
pointing to further increases in 2026.
The value of these systems is well understood, but financing to support their

protection remains a challenge
On a global level, the economic case for protecting these assets is clear, with the World
Bank estimating that more than half of global GDP depends on nature and its services,
while studies put the value of the ocean economy at between $3-6tn. Developing
countries state that while they want to protect oceans and nature, this can often be at
odds with national economic development goals. Finance flows for conservation and

protection initiatives remain relatively small compared with projects in areas such as
clean energy, while developing countries cite misalignment between their needs and
financing conditions, with accessibility a further challenge.
Scaling the financial sector’s involvement will be key
Our analysis shows 2026 is already a record year for blue bond issuance, though it
remains a fraction of green bond issuance. The Ocean Risk and Resilience Action Alliance

(ORRAA), which funds coastal resilience, secured new commitments at COP31, with
Canada pledging CAD$14mn over four years and Australia AUD$1mn over two.
Mangrove Breakthrough, which backs mangrove protection (coastal forests that shield
shorelines, store carbon and support fisheries), said its tracked large-scale projects
reached almost US$1bn in cumulative value since 2020, up 32% year on year.
On carbon markets, implementation is now the main focus for Article 6

Carbon markets let countries and companies offset emissions by paying for projects that
cut or remove them elsewhere. Article 6 of the Paris Agreement sets UN-backed rules for
such trading, and most technical details were settled at earlier COPs. COP31 left the
rulebook intact, with a review due in 2028, so attention turned to implementation. On 16
November the UN climate secretariat soft-launched the first version of an international
registry for Article 6.2, the country-to-country track (Article 6.4 is the centralised UN

mechanism). This international version lets countries without their own registry record
and track credit transfers.
Several Article 6 deals were announced
Norway agreed to buy up to 12 million tonnes of emissions reductions from solar
projects run by Indonesia’s state utility PLN through 2038, at a reported US$20–40 a
tonne. It pays only once reductions are verified and delivered, funding projects that
would otherwise not be viable. Separately, Singapore approved its first Article 6.2 project,

in Rwanda, which will distribute 300,000 ceramic water filters so households can purify
water without boiling it over firewood or charcoal. It is expected to avoid about 780,000
tonnes of CO₂e in 2026–30.
For carbon markets, buyer confidence should remain the priority
Fourteen governments signed the Antalya Statement on Mobilising Private Finance
for Climate Action on 12 November, with a playbook of eight policy levers to grow
corporate demand for high-integrity credits. Corporates and investors still stress the

need for robust credits that deliver the promised emissions reductions and limit
greenwashing risk, while some governments want faster payments and less bureaucracy.
Campaigners warn that lobbying to loosen the rules will intensify before the 2028 review.
In our view, buyer confidence in credit trading processes is paramount and should
remain the priority.
Note: Deutsche Bank is a member of the Ocean Risk and Resilience Action Alliance (ORRAA).

### Page 19

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
F U TU R E O F  C O P
Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027, though questions over how COP will evolve are in focus
COP has shifted from setting targets to implementation. Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027, where we expect a focus on delivering commitments and climate finance access, reflecting African
priorities. COP33 falls to Asia-Pacific in 2028, though the host remains unconfirmed at present. COP32 and COP33 will test how well multilateralism can respond to the setback at COP31. With a

second Global Stocktake concluding in 2028, its outcome should inform countries’ next national climate plans (NDCs, their emissions-reduction pledges), due in 2030.

Appendix A: Key COP terms used in this report
This appendix explains the main COP process terms and negotiating concepts used throughout the report. Definitions are summarised for non-specialist readers and reflect how each term is
used in this deck.
20
TERM DEFINITION
35-by-35 pledge Türkiye’s voluntary goal to lift electricity’s share of global final energy use to 35% by 2035; endorsed by 80+ countries.

Action Agenda Voluntary, non-binding pledges and initiatives launched alongside the formal talks. It is separate from negotiated decisions.
Annex II / Non-Annex I UNFCCC country classification. Annex II covers developed economies; Non-Annex I covers most other countries, including China and India.
Article 6 Paris Agreement framework including rules on international carbon markets. Article 6.2 covers bilateral trading of credits between countries.

Article 9
Paris Agreement finance provisions. Article 9.1 covers developed
countries’ obligation to provide finance. COP30 set a two-year work
programme for 2026-27.
Baku to Belém Roadmap
to $1.3T
Roadmap to scale climate finance to $1.3tn a year by 2035 from all
sources, inclusive of the $300bn NCQG.
Blue Zone UN-controlled area of the COP venue for formal negotiations, pavilions and side events.
Bonn sessions Mid-year technical talks of the UNFCCC Subsidiary Bodies in Bonn, Germany, which prepare the ground for COP discussions.

Brazil fossil fuel roadmap COP30 Presidency’s voluntary global roadmap for transitioning away from fossil fuels, pursued outside the formal UN negotiations.
CBAM Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism: EU levy on imports of carbon- intensive goods such as steel, cement, fertiliser and aluminium.
Coalitions of the willing Like-minded countries acting outside the consensus process, such as the 50+ nation Santa Marta process on fossil fuel roadmaps.
Consensus How COP decisions are taken: adopted when no party formally objects, so a single party can theoretically block or dilute an outcome.

COP / COPs Conference of the Parties: the UNFCCC’s annual decision-making summit, where nearly 200 parties negotiate climate action.
COP Presidency The host country’s team that chairs the conference. For COP31, Türkiye’s Murat Kurum was COP President and led the Action Agenda.
Final COP text / cover text Headline decisions adopted by consensus at the close of the COP, setting its formal outcome. Also called the cover decision.
TERM DEFINITION
Formal agenda Negotiated items on the official COP agenda, decided by consensus. Separate from the voluntary Action Agenda.

Global Goal on
Adaptation (GGA)
Paris Agreement goal to strengthen resilience. COP30 adopted 59
indicators; the process to refine these moved forward at COP31.
Global South Informal term for developing and emerging economies, mainly in Africa, Asia and Latin America.
Global Stocktake (GST) Five-yearly review of progress under the Paris Agreement. The second runs from COP31 to COP33 (2028).
IPCC Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change: the UN body that assesses climate science. Its reports feed the Global Stocktake.

Just transition
mechanism
Belém Action Mechanism agreed at COP30, focused on fairness in the
shift to a low-carbon economy.
Loss and damage fund Fund set up at COP27 and operationalised in 2023 to support vulnerable developing countries.
NCQG New Collective Quantified Goal (COP29): at least $300bn a year for developing countries by 2035, within a $1.3tn ambition.
NDC Nationally determined contribution: each country’s national climate plan, with emissions targets and actions, updated every five years.

Negotiating groups Blocs coordinating national positions: G77 & China, EU, African and Arab Groups, LDCs (least developed) and AOSIS (small island states).
Paris Agreement 2015 treaty to hold warming well below 2°C and pursue 1.5°C, with national climate plans (NDCs) as its core mechanism.
President of Negotiations COP31 role held by Australia’s Chris Bowen, who drafted final texts and steered government-to-government talks.
Temperature overshoot Global warming exceeding the 1.5°C Paris limit, temporarily or permanently, which science now suggests is likely.

UAE Consensus COP28 (Dubai, 2023) agreement to “transition away from fossil fuels”, the first such call in a COP text.
UNFCCC UN Framework Convention on Climate Change: the 1992 treaty behind the climate process. Its parties meet annually at the COP.

### Page 1

After resilient growth this year, several headwinds will likely weigh on the n
economy in 2027. We expect high energy prices to slow consumption over the 
winter, tighter credit conditions to curb demand and France to stagnate until the 
elections. But global manufacturing (supported by AI capex), German fiscal 
expansion and continued structural expansion in Southern Europe should 
cushion these headwinds. We expect Euro area growth of 0.9% in 2027, below 
the recent run rate, estimated potential and consensus.  
High energy prices will likely keep headline inflation near 4% through the winter, n
while indirect effects push core inflation to a 2.9% peak in Q2. But we expect a 
short-lived hump given limited wage second-round effects, with sequential 
inflation pressures largely normalising in 2027H2. 
Above-target inflation and recent ECB commentary point to one more rate hike n
in December. A March move is possible with higher energy prices and stronger 
data, but the hurdle to faster hikes is high. We expect normalisation cuts from 
2027Q4 as the inflation shock fades, leaving our rate path below market pricing. 
Intensifying French budget tensions are the main risk. Polls suggest that Marine n
Le Pen is most likely to become President. If she is elected and wins a majority in 
Parliament, investors could question her commitment to reduce the deficit. If the 
elections fail to deliver a parliamentary majority, the prospects of continued high 
deficits could further weigh on domestic assets. French fiscal tensions could spill 
over into other high-debt countries, but the risk of area-wide contagion looks 
notably lower than in 2011-15. 
Europe also faces structural headwinds from lost competitiveness, rising n
competition from China and demographic changes. Growth industries (such as 
defence), widespread AI adoption and further internal market integration) offer 
significant opportunities, although the upcoming elections will likely hinder 
major reform progress in the near term.

### Page 2

Euro Area 2027 Outlook: Resilience Put to the Test

Following surprisingly resilient Euro area growth this year, we expect 2027 to be more 
challenging. We forecast area-wide growth of 0.9% next year, slightly below the recent 
run rate, our estimate of potential and consensus (Exhibit 1). But we expect growth to 
improve in H2 as headwinds fade and see significant variation across the Euro area, with 
firm growth in Germany and Spain, subdued momentum in Italy and stagnation in 
France.

Rising Headwinds 
We expect several headwinds to slow growth next year, especially in H1. 
First, high energy prices are likely to weigh on consumption over the winter (Exhibit 2). 
Our commodity strategists expect Brent oil and European TTF gas prices to remain 
elevated given continued disruption of Middle East supplies, with risks towards renewed 
escalation and higher prices. As a result, high headline inflation is likely to weigh 
materially on real disposable income over the winter and slow consumer spending. 
Consistent with this, consumer confidence has dropped since the summer and retail 
sales have weakened, especially in Germany.

Exhibit 1: : We Expect Trend-Like Growth in 2027

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, Bloomberg
13 November 2026   2
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 3

Second, tighter credit conditions look set to slow growth (Exhibit 3). Following recent 
easing, our area-wide financial condition index (GSFCI) is now only modestly tighter 
since the summer, as a weaker trade-weighted Euro has mitigated the effects of higher 
long-term yields and wider credit spreads. But survey-based measures of bank lending 
have deteriorated notably, especially in France. As a result, credit conditions are likely to 
act as a moderate headwind to growth next year, particularly on rate-sensitive domestic 
activity (such as housing and business investment).

Third, we expect the French economy to stagnate until the elections (Exhibit 4). In 
addition to tightening financial conditions, heightened political and policy uncertainty 
are likely to lead households and firms to postpone major consumption and investment 
decisions. Fiscal policy is likely to remain a contractionary force, because a large 
increase in interest payments will likely mask a modest consolidation in the primary 
balance. We forecast annual average growth of 0.1% in 2027, well below other

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, Bloomberg

Exhibit 3: Tighter Credit Conditions Will Weigh on Growth

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics
13 November 2026   3
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 4

forecasters and the weakest forecast among the developed economies we cover.

Sources of Resilience 
That said, we see several sources of resilience, which should buffer these headwinds and 
help growth recover in H2. 
First, we expect a continued boost from the global manufacturing cycle (Exhibit 5). The 
international industrial upswing continues, boosting factory orders and exports, 
especially in Germany. AI capex is an important driver behind this pickup, likely lifting 
Euro area growth by 0.1pp this year and next. Given spare capacity in European 
manufacturing—particularly in Germany—we see a continued cyclical export tailwind 
ahead.

Second, fiscal policy will continue to support growth in Germany (Exhibit 6). We remain 
constructive on the German fiscal rollout driven by higher defence spending and

Exhibit 4: We Expect Stagnation in France as Fiscal Uncertainty Builds

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics

Exhibit 5: A Continued Boost from Global Manufacturing, Including AI Capex

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics
13 November 2026   4
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 5

improved execution of infrastructure investment. We expect the German deficit to rise 
to 4.6% next year (from 4% in 2026) and boost German growth by 0.6pp (vs 0.7pp this 
year). That said, fiscal policy looks set to turn contractionary in France and Spain as 
national consolidation pressures build and the Recovery Fund expires. As a result, we see 
a neutral area-wide fiscal stance in 2027 after a small net boost this year, before turning 
contractionary in 2028.

Third, we see continued structural expansion in Southern Europe (Exhibit 7). Spain, in 
particular, has continued to outgrow the Euro area materially, driven by strong gains in 
employment (underpinned by immigration), higher-value-added services (including 
finance and ICT) and investment. While momentum is likely to moderate next year, we 
see continued outperformance in the South.

### Page 6

Modest Underlying Inflation Pressures 
High energy prices (including product spreads) are likely to keep headline inflation close 
to 4% through the winter (Exhibit 8). Headline inflation could climb even higher if upside 
risks to our commodity price forecasts materialise, with headline inflation peaking close 
to 6% in an adverse scenario for oil and gas. We also see building pressures on food 
inflation next year, reflecting passthrough effects from higher energy prices, the ongoing 
Russia/Ukraine war and global weather patterns.

We do not, however, look for material passthrough effects from headline to underlying 
inflation (Exhibit 9). So far, we see little evidence of indirect effects of higher energy 
prices into core inflation, with muted movement in energy-intensive core goods prices. 
Instead, we estimate that most of the recent pickup in core goods inflation reflects 
AI-related equipment. Given pipeline pressures in import and producer prices as well as 
airfares, we expect more notable indirect effects to build in coming months, pushing 
core inflation up.

Exhibit 8: A Sharp Pickup in Headline Inflation

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics
13 November 2026   6
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 7

But we expect the core inflation hump to be short-lived given signs of limited 
second-round effects (Exhibit 10). Measures of wage growth have remained contained 
despite the surge in energy prices, including forward-looking surveys (such as the Indeed 
and ECB wage trackers) and recent wage agreements. While we have slightly raised our 
forecast for nominal wage growth next year, we expect pay gains to return to their 
normal range in 2028, driven by moderating labour demand.

Taken together, we expect core inflation to peak at 2.9% in Q2, significantly above the 
ECB’s target and somewhat above the recent staff projections (Exhibit 11), partly 
reflecting firmer energy price assumptions. But we expect the overshoot to be notably 
more limited and temporary than in 2022-23, with sequential inflation pressures largely 
normalising in 2027H2.

Exhibit 10: Little Risk of Second-Round Effects

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, ECB
